@@106299156 <p> Tucked just behind the tree line in verdant countryside , obscured from the view of passing cars , lies an oddity of Israel . <p> At first glance , the rows of quaint bungalows and winding streets could be a village anywhere -- there is a post office , a swimming pool and a small hotel . <p> But to its roughly 300 residents it represents a utopian project , painstakingly built over half a century , holding the key to peace in a country seemingly perpetually at war . <p> Known in Arabic as Wahat al-Salaam and in Hebrew as Neve Shalom -- translated in English as Oasis of Peace -- it is Israel 's only community shared intentionally between Jews and Palestinians . <p> At the school Israeli and Palestinian children play and learn together . ( ABC News : Tom Joyner ) <p> Its annual budget , council membership and even school curriculum are governed equally between the two groups in the hope it will show other Israelis -- and the wider world -- that a shared society is possible . <h> A shared society <p> Across Israel , Palestinian citizens -- who make up @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ daily institutionalised racism and live largely in communities separate from their Jewish counterparts . <p> But leaders of the Oasis of Peace say the community is an outlier against a national backdrop of violence and hostility toward Palestinians . <p> Co-education directors at the Oasis of Peace , Nir Sharon and Samah Salaimeh , chat on a bench in the school yard . ( ABC News : Tom Joyner ) <p> " It 's working , " said Samah Salaimeh , a Palestinian citizen of Israel , who runs the village 's education system with a Jewish colleague . <p> " Nobody can ignore that . " <p> On the grounds of its primary school , there are no Israeli flags on display , and its carefully balanced mix of students learn side-by-side in classrooms where lessons are taught alternately in Hebrew and Arabic . <p> In history class they are taught Palestinian perspectives , including the Nakba -- the forced displacement of Palestinians by Jews during the creation of Israel in 1948 -- as well as decades of suffering under Israel 's occupation . <p> Long-time teacher Yael Zaltzman @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the Oasis of Peace school . ( ABC News : Tom Joyner ) <p> " It 's not easy all the time , " said Yael Zaltzman , an Israeli Jew who teaches Hebrew . <p> " But this is a dream . I feel like I can change something a little bit in this country . " <p> Seated near the back of a noisy classroom one morning , two girls watched distractedly as Ms Zaltzman pointed to lines of Hebrew text projected onto the whiteboard . <p> A Palestinian girl sits in class next to a Jewish classmate at the Oasis of Peace school . ( ABC News : Tom Joyner ) <p> " Abigail , you gave us a wrong answer , " Ms Zaltzman gently scolded the Jewish girl of the pair . <p> " You now have to listen to what she 's going to say , " she added , gesturing to her neighbour , prompting the Palestinian girl to burst into a fit of giggles . <h> ' The only place I would want to live ' <p> The Oasis sits roughly on @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the Palestinian land it occupies in the West Bank . <p> To reach it , you must take an exit off Highway 1 , a major artery linking Jerusalem with Tel Aviv , and follow a winding road that leads to a turn-off signposted in three languages . <p> A sign at the entrance of the Oasis of Peace pointing visitors towards the school . ( ABC News : Tom Joyner ) <p> " This is the only place I would want to live in in Israel these days , " said Eldad Joffe , a Jew who chairs the local council . <p> Since the Hamas-led attacks on October 7 , the Oasis has found itself caught in its own battle for ideas as fighting continues only two hours away in Gaza . <p> The months of Israel 's military campaign since have caused unease among residents , though in ways different to elsewhere in the country . <p> Two Palestinian boys read books in Arabic as part of language class at the Oasis of Peace school . ( ABC News : Tom Joyner ) <p> Few take notice @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ they say are heading to Gaza to bomb Palestinians . <p> Aya and her best friend , Ahava Raviv , a 12-year-old Jew , both oppose Israel 's measures in Gaza -- Ahava even plans to resist conscription into the army when she turns 18 . <p> " She is close to me . I sincerely believe we will stay like that forever , " Aya added . <p> For Samah Salaimeh , and her co-director of education Nir Sharon , decades of first-hand experiences of Israel 's occupation have imparted lessons on how to provide the best guidance to the children of the Oasis . <p> Long-time teacher Yael Zaltzman ( centre ) corrals a group of excited children from both Palestinian and Jewish backgrounds in her classroom . ( ABC News : Tom Joyner ) <p> " We have a history . We know how to deal with difficult times with the kids , " said Ms Salaimeh , who is the descendent of Palestinian refugees forced by Zionists from their village in 1948 . <p> In settings with children , teaching staff have made sure to emphasise @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ that peace is a viable goal to work towards . <p> " In many ways , I think the children protect us as adults because they maintain normal relationships , " she said . <h> A blueprint for the nation <p> This year marks 54 years since the Oasis was founded by a Dominican priest on agricultural land leased from a nearby monastery . <p> Today , there are some 80 families who live there -- half Palestinian and half Jewish -- and although there are the usual disagreements between neighbours , residents are proud to point out none have ever packed up and left . <p> In fact , a waitlist of newcomers to move there has grown so large that organisers have had to turn some applicants away . <p> Visiting scholars , foreign dignitaries and tourists come to the village to marvel at its success , and remark that such an idea could become a blueprint for the rest of the country to follow . <p> Two Palestinian boys play next to two Jewish girls at lunchtime in the school yard in the Oasis of Peace . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Israel , there 's not the same reverence -- two arson attacks on the village in 2020 badly damaged a school building , while assailants slashed tyres and sprayed graffiti in a 2012 series of hate crimes . <p> With money from liberal-minded foreign donors , who finance the village through fundraising organisations abroad , the school has since been rebuilt . <p> " It 's no secret that we are not that popular , " said Ms Salaimeh . <p> " We are living evidence that peace is possible between Palestinians and Israelis . This government wants everybody to believe in war and killing . " <p> That government , which under Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu is the most right-wing in Israel 's history , takes a dim view of the Oasis , according to its leaders , who say it provides no financial support . <p> On the day the ABC visited , two inspectors from the education ministry -- both from illegal settlements in the occupied West Bank -- caused staff to bristle , though they left without an incident . <p> " We play tango with @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Sometimes we have to have a low profile and we want to keep going and do what we are good at . " <p> A Palestinian actor performs a comedic play in front of the children from the Oasis of Peace school . ( ABC News : Tom Joyner ) <p> One winter 's morning , the school 's children gathered in the hall to watch a performance of an Arabic play , Princess Zomorod , by a visiting troupe from the north of Israel . <p> Palestinian and Jewish students as well as staff watched attentively , enthralled by the story of a Palestinian woman seeking a suitor , breaking at times into roars of laughter . <p> " What I saw here , Arabs and Jews in the same hall -- watching an Arabic play together -- is something wonderful , " said Rayan Rayan , an actor who played the eponymous princess . <p> " This is a good thing because they are raising them with these beliefs . When they grow up , they will keep them and practise them for life . "
